New ideas for handling of loop and angular integrals in D-dimensions in QCD
Valery E. Lyubovitskij, Fabian Wunder, Alexey S. Zhevlakov

Abstract
We discuss new ideas for consideration of loop diagrams and angular integrals in D D -dimensions in QCD. In case of loop diagrams, we propose the covariant formalism of expansion of tensorial loop integrals into the orthogonal basis of linear combinations of external momenta. It gives a very simple representation for the final results and is more convenient for calculations on computer algebra systems. In case of angular integrals we demonstrate how to simplify the integration of differential cross sections over polar angles. Also we derive the recursion relations, which allow to reduce all occurring angular integrals to a short set of basic scalar integrals. All order ε \varepsilon -expansion is given for all angular integrals with up to two denominators based on the expansion of the basic integrals and using recursion relations. A geometric picture for partial fractioning is developed which provides a new rotational invariant algorithm to reduce the number of denominators.



1
Introduction

Evaluation of loop and angular integrals is one of the basic tasks in
quantum field theory. During last five decades huge progress
has been achieved in analytical handling of loop integrals in QCD
(see, e.g., Refs.
tHooft:1972tcz
-
Kalmykov:2020cqz
).
While many different methods for evaluating loop diagrams have been
developed, they mainly use the following techniques:

•

Reduction of tensor structure of loop diagrams using
the Passarino-Veltman (PV) method
Passarino:1978jh

based on Lorentz covariance of matrix elements and fractioning
of the denominators in loop integrals. For extensions of the alternative
PV methods up to six external legs see Ref.
Denner:2005nn
;

•

Reduction of numerators containing virtual momenta by means
of derivatives acting on linear combinations of external
momenta
Davydychev:1991va
;
Tarasov:1996br
;
Fleischer:1999hq
;
Smirnov:2006ry
;
Faessler:2006ft
;
Gutsche:2018msz
;
Zhevlakov:2018rwo
;

•

Reduction of tensor
to scalar integrals with shifted dimension
Davydychev:1991va
,
which further reduce to the loop integrals
in generic dimension
Tarasov:1996br
;
Fleischer:1999hq
;

•

using helicity methods in evaluating loop integrals
Korner:1990sj
;
Korner:1982vg
;

•

Method based on integration by parts (IBP) reduction
is widely used for handling two and higher loops integrals
Laporta:2001dd
;

•

Methods based on decomposition of Lorentz vectors
in a parallel and orthogonal space in the context of one-loop
and multi-loop integrand reduction, and generalized
unitarity
Bern:1994zx
;
Bern:1994cg
;
Britto:2004nc
;
Ellis:2007br
;
Ossola:2006us
;
Forde:2007mi
;
Giele:2008ve
;
Peraro:2020sfm
;

•

Development of analytical and numerical methods for
\varepsilon
-expansion of
loop integrals (see, e.g., Refs.
Kalmykov:2006pu
;
Kalmykov:2020cqz
).

In particular, the PV method
Passarino:1978jh
is based
on expanding integrals, which contain the loop momentum in the numerator,
in terms of a set of scalar functions (form factors) multiplying
a basis of tensors constructed from external momenta.
The form factors can be expressed by the readily calculated scalar bubble,
triangle and box one-loop integrals. The scalar form factors of tensorial
loop integrals are obtained algebraically by solving a system of linear
equations. This leads to the introduction of the Gram determinant composed of
scalar products of external momenta. As correctly stressed
in Ref.
Fleischer:1999hq
the usage of the PV method is best suited
for diagrams with four or less external legs, otherwise the tensor structure
of the diagrams with multi-legs becomes more complicated due to solving systems
of many algebraic equations and due to vanishing Gram determinants at some
kinematic cases.

Success of the PV method
Passarino:1978jh
stimulated further development
of the idea to use the combinations of external momenta for the construction
of basis for the expansion of tensorial loop integrals. In particular,
the decomposition of Lorentz vectors in a parallel and
orthogonal space has already been extensively used in the context of one-loop
and multi-loop integrand reduction, generalized unitarity and recent developments
in the decomposition of amplitudes into tensors and form factors (see,
e.g., discussion in Refs.
Ossola:2006us
;
Forde:2007mi
;
Peraro:2020sfm
).
In Ref.
Denner:2005nn
the PV method was extended using alternative
techniques up to six external legs.

In the present paper we introduce the following improvement of the PV method.
Instead of expanding loop integrals in the trivial basis of occurring external
momenta we perform the expansion in the basis of orthogonal linear combinations
of the external momenta. It allows us to exclude the stage of algebraically solving
a system of equations in order to pin down the scalar functions in which we expand tensor
loop diagrams. Instead we derive these scalar functions straightforwardly using
fractioning relations involving inverse denominators in loop integrals.

We consider only loop integrals without referring to specific type
of the particles propagating in the loop. The proposed formalism can be adapted to full
amplitudes.

Our method is very useful for a convenient analytic treatment of processes with massless particles, e.g., Drell-Yan (DY)
and SIDIS processes, prompt photon and heavy flavor production, etc. However, it is important to notice
that the proposed method can be generalized to arbitrary number of legs in one-loop
diagrams and to massive particles.

While for many cutting-edge perturbative studies, like inclusive DY processes at
N^{3}
LO
(see, e.g., Refs.
Duhr:2020seh
;
Duhr:2020sdp
), multi-loop techniques are key, we would like to stress that studies where a refinement of one-loop techniques can provide considerable improvement are still an active area of research.

For understanding angular distributions, spin effects and for access
to the parton distributions (PDFs and TMDs), improving on the existing tools in producing analytical
expressions as simply and economically as possible is beneficial.
Besides the importance of analytical methods for phenomenological study of QCD processes
in the fixed target regime (DY, SIDIS, etc.), handy analytical expressions are also important
for performing resummation of large logarithms, verifying of different identities (like Lam-Tung
relation) involving hadronic structure functions, behavior of the hadronic amplitudes at different
kinematical limits and their expansions in small parameters (like small transverse momentum of
photon, etc.). See, e.g., discussion in Refs.
Boer:2006eq
;
Berger:2007jw
.

In the second part of our paper we discuss new ideas concerning angular
integration in QCD
vanNeerven:1985xr
-
Anastasiou:2002yz
.
We extend the known closed results for massless and single massive two denominator
integrals in
D
dimensions to the double massive case.
All order
\varepsilon
-expansion is given for all angular integrals with up to
two denominators. A geometric picture for partial fractioning is developed which provides
a new rotational invariant algorithm to reduce the number of denominators.

For the calculation of real emission corrections in higher order perturbative calculations
the phase space integration (PSI) constitutes the most challenging part. When massless external
particles are involved, the integrals are singular, thus numerical integration is not feasible.
To regularize the divergent integrals, analytic integration methods are necessary.
A prominent class of divergences are those of collinear type.
They appear if two external massless particles propagate in the same direction making
their scalar product vanish. These singularities appear in the angular PSI.
Henceforth, analytic methods for their calculation are essential.

Since its introduction, the dimensional regularization (DR)
tHooft:1972tcz
by t’Hooft
and Veltman is the preferred regularization method in gauge theories. In particular,
it gives the straightforward and consistent recipe to perform QCD calculations in
D

dimensions and take the physical limit
D\rightarrow 4
only after the cancellation of
all divergences. Throughout the paper we choose the most common definition of
D=4-2\varepsilon
.
Note that some of the references use
D=4\pm\varepsilon
instead.
The collinear divergences appearing in angular PSI manifest themselves
as poles in the Laurent expansion about
\varepsilon=0
.

Angular integrals appear in the calculation of the two, three and four particle PSIs
and thus appear in a plethora of perturbative calculations. QCD examples include
processes such as
Drell-Yan
Schellekens:1981kq
;
matsuura1989calculation
;
matsuura1990contribution
;
hamberg1991complete
;
Mirkes:1992hu
;
Bahjat-Abbas:2018hpv
,
deep inelastic scattering (DIS)
duke1982quantum
,
semi-inclusive DIS (SIDIS)
Anderle:2016kwa
;
Wang:2019bvb
,
prompt photon production
Gordon:1993qc
,
hadron-hadron scattering
ellis1980large
, and
heavy quark production
Beenakker:1988bq
;
Bojak:2000eu
.

In the literature those angular integrals were mostly considered in terms
of the integrals, which we call in our paper the

Neerven integrals

vanNeerven:1985xr
;
Beenakker:1988bq
:

\displaystyle I^{j,l}_{D}=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\frac{1}{(a+b\cos\theta_{1})^{j}(A+B\cos\theta_{1}+C\sin\theta_{1}\cos\theta_{2})^{l}}\,,

(1)

with

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\sin^{D-3}\theta_{1}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\sin^{D-4}\theta_{2}.

(2)

They are divided in four classes depending on whether the conditions
a^{2}=b^{2}
and
A^{2}=B^{2}+C^{2}
hold.
We will call
I^{(j,l)}_{D}

massless
, if both equations are satisfied and
single massive
,
if one of them holds and else
double massive
. The latter are finite for
D=4
, the other
two involve singularities.

We will start our discussion with an overview of how those integral were used in the older
literature. From their introduction in the early 80s onwards they were an important ingredient
for calculations at the NNLO level. After providing context on the development of the knowledge
about angular integrals, we discuss how the use of angular integrals compares to the method
of
reversed unitarity
, which is in frequent use today. This will come along with a review
of modern application of angular integrals, which demonstrates that, thus known for four decades
in QCD calculations, angular integrals are still in use today and a more systematic study of these
was long overdue.

First application of DR for treatment of divergences in the angular PSIs
was performed by Ellis et al. in Ref.
ellis1980large
.
In particular, angular integrals
\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}
as part of
the phase space
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{PS}_{3}
were considered and
the massless integrals
I^{(1,1)}_{D}
were introduced, to which all appearing PSIs could be
reduced
“by suitable rotation and partial fractioning”
.
In a similar fashion Ref.
duke1982quantum
treated the three particle PSI.
In Ref.
duke1982quantum
partial fractioning was introduced
using identities involving the Mandelstam variables in order to reduce the PSIs
to the massless
I^{(1,1)}_{D}
and
I^{(1,0)}_{D}
.
Ref.
devoto1984analytic
extended the calculation of angular integrals to the single
massive
I^{(1,1)}_{D}
, while a list of the double massive integrals

I^{(j,l)}_{4}
for
-2\leq j,l\leq 2
restricted to the four-dimensional case
has been derived in Ref.
Schellekens:1981kq
.

In this vein, major achievement was Neerven’s analytic calculation of
the massless integrals
I^{(j,l)}_{D}
in
Ref.
vanNeerven:1985xr
. By calculating them as the discontinuity of a box graph and
employing the optical theorem he heavily influenced the approach towards PSIs.
For a long time this constituted the only published detailed calculation of angular integrals.
Other papers were heavily based on the results of Ref.
vanNeerven:1985xr
.
The most commonly used reference for the angular integrals for the last three decades
is
Beenakker:1988bq
. Ref.
Beenakker:1988bq
gave the angular integral
in the form of Eq. (
1
) using the set of parameters

\{a,b,A,B,C\}
and produced a comprehensive, often cited list of angular integrals.
The double massive integral
I^{(j,l)}_{4}
was given for the case
|j|,|l|\leq 2
.
The divergent integrals were expanded up to order
\varepsilon
, the massless integral
was given for
j=1,-2\leq l\leq 1
, and the single massive
for
l=1
,
j=-2,-1,1,2
and
l=2
,
j=0,1,2
. The single massive integrals were given in two groups
without making the symmetry between
a^{2}\neq b^{2}
and
A^{2}\neq B^{2}+C^{2}
explicit.
The list of the integrals was based on the calculation method put forward
in Refs.
vanNeerven:1985xr
;
Schellekens:1981kq
;
devoto1984analytic
.
A similar list was compiled in Ref.
Smith:1989xz
.
Some early use of those lists can be found in Ref.
matsuura1989calculation
.
Ref.
matsuura1989calculation
mentioned the massless and single massive integrals
I^{(j,l)}_{D}

and stated that the single massive integral
“cannot be written in as elegant a form as”

the massless integral, hence
“brute force”
methods were used.
Ref.
matsuura1989calculation
derived an early version of two-point
partial fractioning discussed in more details in Appendix
D

of the present paper. Ref.
matsuura1990contribution
considered the angular
integration as part of the three-particle phase space. Again Eq. (
1
)
appeared for the massless case. Note that only two-point partial fractioning was mentioned in
Ref.
matsuura1989calculation
and there was
no three-point partial fractioning (see Appendix
D
).
So seven integrals from the list of Ref.
matsuura1989calculation
,
including those containing the massive propagator, were said to be integrated
by
“brute force methods”
. In the follow-up paper
hamberg1991complete

the Neerven integral came with a reference to the 1989 paper
Beenakker:1988bq
.
The single massive integral was described as
“very cumbersome”
. About the double massive case they
write
“fortunately [the double massive integral] can be avoided by choosing an appropriate frame.”

As a benchmark, their partial fractioning algorithm led to 217 different 3-body PSI.

Later, in Ref.
Mirkes:1992hu
the Neerven integral
was considered as part of a four-body phase space by Mirkes.
The three classes of angular integrals he lists correspond to massless and single-massive
integrals. Some of the single massive integrals were expanded up to order
\varepsilon
.
The recursion given for his class III integrals without reference
fail for
\varepsilon\neq 0
. We correct this shortcoming in Sec.
3.5.4
.
The partial fractioning was described as
“very involved”
.
In Ref.
Gordon:1993qc
the Neerven integral together with
“extensive use of relations
between Mandelstam variables”
were employed for partial fractioning
to calculate the 3-body phase space of prompt photon production. Additional phase space factors
were accommodated for by dimensional shift
\varepsilon\rightarrow\varepsilon-1
. Such applications motivate to publish
not only
\varepsilon
-expansions but also general results permitting for expansion about other values of
\varepsilon
.
We consider algebraic identities to perform dimensional shifts in Sec.
3.3.2
.
Further consideration of the Neerven type integrals in the context of the three particle phase space has
been considered in Ref.
Bojak:2000eu
. The
“extensive partial fractioning”
based on Mandelstam
identities
“though computerized […] has the disadvantage of often yielding unnecessarily complicated
expressions”
. It leads to massless, single-massive and double-massive Neerven integrals.
The symmetry between the two classes of single-massive integrals was recognized and relations between
the double-massive integrals via differentiating with respect to
a
and
A
were given
(compare
3.5.3
).

In the decade following Neervens paper, there was not much improvement on the treatment
of angular integrals. In 2001 Anastasiou and Melnikov
Anastasiou:2002yz
proposed
a new method called reverse unitarity for calculations of phase space
integrals. Expressing these integrals in terms of loop integrals made them accessible
through the sophisticated mathematical methods developed for handling of loop integrals such as
integration-by-parts relations, differential equations, etc.
This approach computes cut diagrams as solutions of differential
equations
Argeri:2007up
;
Moch:2007jk
. Technically the latter are needed to be augmented with
boundary conditions coming from a separate calculation.
Analytic results are obtained in terms of harmonic polylogarithms order by order in
\varepsilon
.
During the last twenty years these methods received an evolution
Mitov:2005ps
;
Bonocore:2016doa
.
The method of reverse unitarity has certain advantages.
It is applicable to rather complicated PSIs since it takes full advantage of the knowledge
about loop integrals. Since every delta function in the phase space translates to additional
propagators, the technique is especially suited for the treatment of inclusive PSIs.
Limitations are induced by the
development of technical methods
for calculation of loop integrals. Difficulties in these approaches grow with the inclusion of
more particles in the finals state, multiple scales and massive particles.

The success of methods based on reversed unitarity provides an explanation of the scarcity
of improvements regarding angular integrals. However, the direct approach towards angular
integrals, rather than treating all PSIs by reversed unitarity still has its merits for certain
applications. It is conceptually simple, since it makes straightforward use of their properties
rather than taking a detour to loop integrals. One might compare the calculation of the massless Neerven integral
in Ref.
vanNeerven:1985xr
to ours in Sec.
3.2.4
as an example. By considering a specific class of PSIs,
in the present paper angular integrals with two denominators, analytic results can be obtained
in closed form and expanded to all orders in
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, recursion relations
can explicitly be solved. This yields a completely solved building block which can be plugged
into calculations, without the need to run an IBP reduction and to solve differential equations
order by order. The expansion presented in Sec.
3.4
has the benefit
to be compatible with commonly used methods since the result is expressed in terms of multiple
polylogarithms. Of course, employing the angular integral discussed here is only applicable for
observables with phase space integrals of appropriate form and is not simply generalizable for
other PSIs. However, the ideas presented should also be of value for related PSIs like angular integrals with 3 and
more denominators. Such integrals appear, e.g., as master integrals in the reversed unitarity
method (see Ref.
Anastasiou:2013srw
).

After introducing the angular integral in its historical context, we will look
at modern developments and usage in the following.
In 2011 Somogyi
Somogyi:2011ir
was the first who employed the property of rotational invariance for
handling angular integrals. He did the first systematic calculation of the angular integrals with
a parametrization independent of the choice of coordinate axis. Furthermore, his approach is much more
straightforward than Neerven’s method. Ref.
Somogyi:2011ir
gave the first closed result for the
single-massive integral in terms of the Appell function. However, for the double massive integral
Ref.
Somogyi:2011ir
can only give a Mellin-Barnes representation and no closed formula
in terms of hypergeometric functions. Ref.
Somogyi:2011ir
lists
the massless integrals up to order
\varepsilon
, the single massive and double massive
to finite order. For the double massive integral the order
\varepsilon
term could not be derived using
the Mellin-Barnes methods since it involved complicated triple sums. Unpublished work towards
the order
\varepsilon
by other means is cited to also
“not have an analytic
answer”

SmithUnpublished
. In the present paper we will present this so far missing result in
Sec.
3.2.6
.

The closed analytic result for the single massive integral was first used
in Ref.
Bolzoni:2010bt
for the calculation of subtraction terms in QCD jet cross sections
at NNLO and more recently in Ref.
Lionetti:2018gko
for subtraction terms in Higgs production.
The single massive integral found further application in Ref.
Specchia:2018uyj
to pin down
phase space master integrals. Also the N
3
LO study of Higgs production
in Ref.
Anastasiou:2013srw
employed Somogyi’s new results. A brought discussion of techniques
for phase-space calculations, emphasizing the benefits of a rotational invariant approach based on
Somogyi’s methods, is given in Ref.
Hoschele:2015whb
. Ref.
Bahjat-Abbas:2018hpv

on the Drell-Yan process at N
3
LO involves both the Neerven integral and Somogyis findings
in the determination of phase space integrals in the soft region. Furthermore angular integrals
in the Somogyi parametrization make their appearance in the determination of beam and soft functions
(see Ref.
Baranowski:2020xlp
).

However, many recent investigations are still based on the rather old Neerven list.
The angular integral in Neerven parametrization appears in
Ringer:2015chp

and
Ringer:2015oaa
as part of the three particle phase space of single spin asymmetry
in
W
-boson production. Ref.
Schlegel:2012ve
employed it for similar purpose.
In Ref.
Anderle:2016kwa
the angular integral is used in Neerven form and some additional
integrals wih higher indices are provided. Ref.
Kotlarski:2016zhv
employs normalized
Neerven integrals for a study of supersymmetry. Further examples of recent uses of Neervens integral
list are such diverse topics as the investigation of twist-3 single-spin
asymmetry
Hinderer:2017bya
, heavy flavour production
Hekhorn:2019nlf
,
or the study of Kaluza-Klein gluons in Ref.
Lillard:2016jxz
.
The present paper is aimed at providing a long overdue update to the still frequently used
tool of angular integration for future studies similar to those mentioned here.

The following three perturbative studies are further examples of recent practice where the presented
method could have been conveniently applied. Ref.
Wang:2019bvb
considers the Neerven integral
as part of the three-particle phase space of NNLO real corrections to SIDIS at high transverse momentum.
The symmetry of single massive integrals is noticed by change of frame, a closed form for the massless
integral is given however it is stated that
“the integral no longer has a closed form”

in the single massive case. They give a detailed description of their partial fractioning algorithm
based on Mandelstam identities and provide an extensive list of integrals.
A recent paper on
e^{+}e^{-}
-annihilation gives the double massive integral
I^{(j,l)}_{4}
for

1\leq j,l\leq 2
and states
“we were not able to find a closed form
in
d
dimensions”

Blumlein:2020jrf
. Their Eq. (151)-(153) constitute two-point partial
fractioning, eq. (154)-(159) is a version of three-point partial fractioning.
In a recent publication on
QED corrections in exclusive

\overline{B}\to\overline{K}{\mathrm{\ell}}^{+}{\mathrm{\ell}}^{-}

decay
Isidori:2020acz
, the order
\varepsilon
of the double massive angular integral was explicitly needed to extract collinear logarithms from soft integrals. Private communication with Somogyi is cited to obtain a result. This showcases the need to improve on the existing literature on angular integrals.

The broad range of applications of the methods for dealing with angular integrals in QCD processes
motivates a fresh study of this problem to supplement the toolbox for PSI.
In particular, one of the main purposes of our paper is to present the angular integrals in a systematic
fashion based on the Somogyi framework using relativistically invariant variables
(scalar products of four momenta/velocities)
Somogyi:2011ir
.

Since the appearance of the angular integrals over a small number of propagators is intimately connected to
partial fractioning, we also provide an algorithm which directly leads to the angular integrals in the form
introduced in Sec.
3.2
. This has the benefit that partial fractioning becomes
manifestly invariant under rotation and leads to considerably short expressions without the need to choose
suitable coordinate axis, simplifying computer implementation.

The systematic approach offers a cleaner picture on the possible types of integrals appearing.
Furthermore, we naturally obtain an expression for the double massive integral in
D
dimensions with
integer coefficients
j
,
l
in terms of single massive integrals, not known in the existing literature.
The general Neerven integral
with integer valued
j
and
l
is calculated in
D
dimensions and expanded to all orders in
\varepsilon

for the first time. The whole calculation is presented in a pedagogical form to encourage further studies
and supplement the existing extensive lists of integrals with straightforward derivations.

The paper is structured as follows.
In Sec.
2
we discuss novel ideas for handling of
loop tensorial integrals in
D
dimensions using the basis of orthogonal external momenta.
In Sec.
3
we discuss new ideas concerning angular integration
in
D
dimensions. Sec.
4
contains our conclusions and summary of the main results.
Technical details of calculations, a discussion of parial fractioning in PSIs, and Tables of angular integrals
are placed in Appendices.

2
New formalism for reduction of tensor loop integrals

In this section we discuss our newly developed formalism for
handling of loop tensorial integrals in
D
dimensions
using an orthogonal basis of external momenta.
As we stressed in Introduction we develop analytic method for
evaluation of one-loop diagrams with its further application to QCD
processes at fixed target regime (DY and SIDIS processes, prompt photon and
heavy flavor production, etc.). Our method can be extended to arbitrary number of legs in one-loop
diagrams and for both massless and massive particles. Development of analytical methods for study of
QCD processes is very important for understanding of their different aspects: angular distributions,
spin effects, access to the parton distributions. It is also important for performing resummation of large
logarithms, verifying of different identities (like Lam-Tung relation) involving hadronic structure functions,
behavior of the hadronic amplitudes at different kinematical limits and their expansions in small parameters
(like small transverse momentum of photon, etc.).
See, e.g., discussion in Refs.
Boer:2006eq
;
Berger:2007jw
.

In present paper we restrict to consideration of
one-loop integral with 2, 3, and 4 external legs and
with specific kinematic relevant for QCD processes at fixed target
regime. Typical loop diagrams
with 2, 3, and 4 external legs are shown in Fig.
1
.

Figure 1:
Loop diagrams: bubble (a), triangle (b) and (c), and box (d).

First, we define the 9 possible denominators occurring in these
loop integrals:

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}=k^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{1}=(k+p_{1})^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{2}=(k-p_{2})^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{3}=(k-k_{1})^{2}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}=(k-q)^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{5}=(k+p_{1}-k_{1})^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{6}=(k+p_{1}-q)^{2}\,,\quad

(3)

\displaystyle\Delta_{7}=(k+p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{8}=(k+q)^{2}\,,\quad\Delta_{9}=(k+k_{1})^{2}\,.

Our formalism is based on the PV technique.
We improve it by expanding the tensor loop integrals in an orthogonal
basis of vectors instead of plain external momenta.
In case of four external particles the orthogonal basis is
specified as following. Let
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
be the momenta of incoming
massless particles with
p_{1}^{2}=p_{2}^{2}=0

and let
k_{1}
and
q
be the momenta of outcoming
particles with massless
k_{1}^{2}=0

and massive/virtual
q^{2}=Q^{2}
. They obey
four-momentum conservation
p_{1}+p_{2}=k_{1}+q
.
The set of Mandelstam variables is specified as:

\displaystyle s=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=(k_{1}+q)^{2}\,,

\displaystyle t=(p_{1}-q)^{2}=(p_{2}-k_{1})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle u=(p_{1}-k_{1})^{2}=(p_{2}-q)^{2}\,,

\displaystyle s+t+u=q^{2}=Q^{2}\,.

(4)

Now we define the basis of 3 orthogonal momenta as

\displaystyle P^{\mu}=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{\mu}\,,\quad R^{\mu}=(p_{1}-p_{2})^{\mu}\,,\quad T^{\mu}=k_{1}^{\mu}-P^{\mu}\,\frac{Pk_{1}}{P^{2}}-R^{\mu}\,\frac{Rk_{1}}{R^{2}}\,,

(5)

where

P^{2}=-R^{2}=s
,
T^{2}=-\dfrac{ut}{s}
and

P\cdot R=P\cdot T=R\cdot T=0
.
Also we introduce the perpendicular metric
tensor
g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
,
which is manifestly orthogonal to all basis momenta:

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}=g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{P^{\mu}P^{\nu}}{P^{2}}-\frac{R^{\mu}R^{\nu}}{R^{2}}-\frac{T^{\mu}T^{\nu}}{T^{2}}\,,

(6)

which satisfies

g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,g_{\mu\nu}^{\perp}=D-3
,

g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,P_{\mu}=g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,R_{\mu}=g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,T_{\mu}=0
.
All above formulas can be extended to the case of a massive
particle with momentum
k_{1}
. The only difference is
that
T^{2}=\dfrac{Q^{2}k_{1}^{2}-ut}{s}
.
The use of such basis considerably simplifies obtaining
all scalar functions parameterizing the loop integrals.

Note, that an extension to an arbitrary number of external and massive
particles is straightforward. In particular, if we consider the scattering of two
particles with momenta
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
producing
n
particles
in the final state with momenta
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}
, then
the orthogonal basis involving
(n+1)
momenta
\{P_{1},\ldots P_{n+1}\}
is
specified as

\displaystyle P_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\mu}\,,\qquad\hskip 49.79231ptP=p_{1}+p_{2}\,,

\displaystyle P_{2}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{\mu}-P^{\mu}\,\frac{PR}{P^{2}}\,,\qquad R=p_{1}-p_{2}\,,

\displaystyle P_{3}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{1}^{\mu}-P_{1}^{\mu}\,\frac{P_{1}k_{1}}{P_{1}^{2}}-P_{2}^{\mu}\,\frac{P_{2}k_{2}}{P_{2}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots

\displaystyle P_{n+1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{n-1}^{\mu}-P_{1}^{\mu}\,\frac{P_{1}k_{n-1}}{P_{1}^{2}}-P_{2}^{\mu}\,\frac{P_{2}k_{n-1}}{P_{2}^{2}}-\ldots-P_{n}^{\mu}\,\frac{P_{n}k_{n-1}}{P_{n}^{2}}\,.

(7)

For the loop integrals with
n
propagators
and with a product of
m
external momenta in numerator we use the notation:

\displaystyle I^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{m}}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots k^{\alpha_{m}}}{\Delta_{i_{1}}\ldots\Delta_{i_{n}}}\,,

(8)

where
\mu
is the renormalization scale.
Below we list the results for bubble, triangle, and box integrals
with kinematics [see Eqs. (
2
)-(
6
)]
specific for the QCD processes at fixed target regime at NNLO.

2.1
Bubble integrals

All bubble integrals are expressed through the scalar
bubble PV function
B_{0}(p^{2})

\displaystyle B_{0}(p^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\frac{1}{k^{2}(k+p)^{2}}

(9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{\frac{D}{2}-2}\,\frac{\Gamma\Big(2-\frac{D}{2}\Big)\,\Gamma^{2}\Big(\frac{D}{2}-1\Big)}{\Gamma\Big(D-2\Big)}\,\biggl(-\frac{p^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\biggr)^{\frac{D}{2}-2}\,,

where
\Gamma(x)
is the gamma function.
The vector bubble integrals are given by:

\displaystyle B_{1}^{\mu}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\frac{k^{\mu}}{k^{2}(k+p)^{2}}=-\frac{p^{\mu}}{2}\,B_{0}(p^{2})\,,

(10)

\displaystyle B_{2}^{\mu\nu}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\frac{k^{\mu}\,k^{\nu}}{k^{2}(k+p)^{2}}=\frac{1}{4(D-1)}\,\biggl(p^{\mu}p^{\nu}\,D-g^{\mu\nu}p^{2}\biggr)\,B_{0}(p^{2})

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\,\biggl[p^{\mu}p^{\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;2}\frac{p^{2}}{D-1}\biggr]\,B_{0}(p^{2})\,,

where

g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;2}=g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu}/p^{2}

is the perpendicular metric tensor for the bubble diagram
with the property:

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;2}\,g_{\perp;2;\mu\nu}=D-1\,.

(12)

Here and in the following, we introduce three orthogonal metric
tensors, one for each specific diagram — bubble, triangle, and box —
which will be labeled by the indices 2, 3, and 4, respectively.
Also, we will see that the following normalization
condition for the metric tensors holds:

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;n}\,g_{\perp;n;\mu\nu}=D+1-n\,.

(13)

For specific choices of momenta the scalar bubble integrals

I_{ij}\equiv B_{0}^{ij}
read:

\displaystyle B_{0}(0)=B_{0}^{01}=B_{0}^{02}=B_{0}^{03}=B_{0}^{09}\,,

\displaystyle B_{0}(u)=B_{0}^{05}\,,\quad B_{0}(t)=B_{0}^{06}\,,\quad B_{0}(s)=B_{0}^{07}\,,\quad B_{0}(Q^{2})=B_{0}^{04}=B_{0}^{08}\,.

(14)

Tensorial bubble integrals for specific momenta follow from
Eqs. (
10
) and (
11
). Note that in case of bubble
diagrams we work with a single transverse vector. Next,
in case of triangle and box diagrams we will use
two and three transverse vectors. However, these perpendicular
vectors can be always expanded in a linear combination
of the vectors of the basis (
5
).

2.2
Triangle integrals

For the triangle integrals (scalar, vector, tensor rank-2)
we use the following definitions, respectively:

\displaystyle I_{ijk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\,\Delta_{j}\,\Delta_{k}}\,,

\displaystyle I_{ijk}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}}{\Delta_{i}\,\Delta_{j}\,\Delta_{k}}\,,

(15)

\displaystyle I_{ijk}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{\Delta_{i}\,\Delta_{j}\,\Delta_{k}}\,.

The scalar integrals
I_{ijk}\equiv C_{0}^{ijk}
obey the following relations
and are expressed through the PV triangle scalar integrals
C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2})
as:

\displaystyle C_{0}(s)=C_{0}^{012}=C_{0}^{017}\,,

\displaystyle C_{0}(t)=C_{0}^{023}=C_{0}^{026}=C_{0}^{069}\,,

\displaystyle C_{0}(u)=C_{0}^{015}=C_{0}^{019}\,,

(16)

\displaystyle C_{0}(s,Q^{2})=C_{0}^{038}=C_{0}^{049}=C_{0}^{078}=C_{0}^{079}\,,

\displaystyle C_{0}(t,Q^{2})=C_{0}^{016}=C_{0}^{018}=C_{0}^{046}\,,

\displaystyle C_{0}(u,Q^{2})=C_{0}^{024}=C_{0}^{025}=C_{0}^{035}=C_{0}^{058}\,.

Here, the integral
C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
is
defined as

\displaystyle C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{1}{k^{2}\,(k+l_{1})^{2}\,(k+l_{2})^{2}}\,,

(17)

where
s_{1}=l_{1}^{2}
,
s_{2}=l_{2}^{2}
,
s_{3}=l_{3}^{2}=(l_{1}-l_{2})^{2}
,
and
2l_{1}l_{2}=s_{1}+s_{2}-s_{3}
.

It is fully symmetric under all permutations of its arguments

\displaystyle C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=C_{0}(s_{1},s_{3},s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{0}(s_{2},s_{3},s_{1})=C_{0}(s_{2},s_{1},s_{3})

\displaystyle=C_{0}(s_{3},s_{1},s_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{0}(s_{3},s_{2},s_{1})\,.

(18)

In specific limit, when one of the arguments is equal to zero,
it is expressed through the bubble PV integral
B_{0}(s)
as:

\displaystyle C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2},0)=\frac{2\,(D-3)}{4-D}\ \frac{B_{0}(s_{1})-B_{0}(s_{2})}{s_{1}-s_{2}}\,.

(19)

Now we consider tensorial triangle integrals.
In contrast to the bubble diagram case, here we introduce
a “perpendicular basis” for the triangle master integral containing
two vectors. These two can be related
to our “orthogonal basis” containing the momenta

P
,
R
,
T
used to parametrize all considered
types of loop diagrams — bubble, triangle, box.

The master vector triangle integral reads

\displaystyle C_{1}^{\mu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}}{k^{2}\,(k+l_{1})^{2}\,(k+l_{2})^{2}}\,.

(20)

For handling triangle diagrams the
perpendicular basis reads:

\displaystyle l_{1\perp}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l_{1}^{\mu}-l_{2}^{\mu}\,\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{l_{1}\,l_{2}}\,,

\displaystyle l_{2\perp}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l_{2}^{\mu}-l_{1}^{\mu}\,\frac{l_{2}^{2}}{l_{1}\,l_{2}}\,,

(21)

with the following properties:

\displaystyle l_{i\perp}\cdot l_{i}=0\,,\quad i=1,2\,,

\displaystyle l_{i\perp}^{2}=-\frac{l_{i}^{2}}{4\,(l_{1}\,l_{2})^{2}}\,\lambda(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})\,\,,\quad i=1,2\,,

\displaystyle l_{1\perp}\cdot l_{2\perp}=\frac{\lambda(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}{4l_{1}l_{2}},

\displaystyle(l_{1\perp}\cdot l_{2\perp})^{2}=\frac{(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}{l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}}\ l_{1\perp}^{2}\,l_{2\perp}^{2}\,,

(22)

where

\displaystyle\lambda(x,y,z)=x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}-2xy-2yz-2xz\,,

(23)

is the kinematic triangle Källen function.
In addition, to accompany the perpendicular basis,
we introduce orthogonal metric tensor

g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}
, which is defined
as

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}=g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{l_{1\perp}^{\mu}l_{1\perp}^{\nu}l_{2}^{2}}{(l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp})^{2}}-\frac{l_{2\perp}^{\mu}l_{2\perp}^{\nu}l_{1}^{2}}{(l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp})^{2}}-\frac{(l_{1\perp}^{\mu}l_{2\perp}^{\nu}+l_{2\perp}^{\mu}l_{1\perp}^{\nu})l_{1}l_{2}}{(l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp})^{2}}\,,

(24)

and has the following properties

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}\cdot l_{i\perp;\mu}=g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}\cdot l_{i\perp;\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}\cdot l_{i;\mu}=g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}\cdot l_{i;\nu}=0\,,\quad i=1,2\,,

(25)

and

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}\cdot g_{\perp;3;\mu\nu}=D-2\,.

(26)

Note, that such choice of basis is free of soft
singularities occurring in the limit
l_{1}^{2}
and/or
l_{2}^{2}=0
.
In particular, in this limit
l_{i\perp}\to l_{i}
.
Also it gives the straightforward results for the scalar
functions in the expansion of tensorial triangle diagrams,
i.e. without the need of solving a system of equations like
in the original PV method.

The expansion of the integral

C_{1}^{\mu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
in the basis reads

\displaystyle C_{1}^{\mu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=l_{1\perp}^{\mu}\,C_{1;l1}\,+\,l_{2\perp}^{\mu}\,C_{1;l2}\,,

(27)

where the scalar functions
C_{1;l1}
and
C_{1;l2}
are fixed
by contraction of our loop integral with
l_{2}
and
l_{1}
,
respectively:

\displaystyle C_{1;l1}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=\frac{1}{l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp}}\,C_{1}^{\mu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\,l_{2\mu}\,,

\displaystyle C_{1;l2}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=\frac{1}{l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp}}\,C_{1}^{\mu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\,l_{1\mu}\,.

(28)

Analytic expressions for the functions
C_{1;l1}
and
C_{1;l2}
read

\displaystyle C_{1;l1}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})=\frac{2l_{1}l_{2}}{\lambda}\,\biggl[B_{0}(s_{1})-B_{0}(s_{3})-s_{2}\,C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle C_{1;l2}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}(s_{2},s_{1},s_{3})=\frac{2l_{1}l_{2}}{\lambda}\,\biggl[B_{0}(s_{2})-B_{0}(s_{3})-s_{1}\,C_{0}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\biggr]\,.

(29)

Note, that
C_{1;l1}
and
C_{1;l2}
are related via exchange of
momenta
l_{1}\leftrightarrow l_{2}
or
s_{1}\leftrightarrow s_{2}
.

By analogy we pin down triangle tensor rank-2 integral

\displaystyle C_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{k^{2}\,(k+l_{1})^{2}\,(k+l_{2})^{2}}

(30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}\,C_{2;g}+l_{1\perp}^{\mu}l_{1\perp}^{\nu}\,C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}+l_{2\perp}^{\mu}l_{2\perp}^{\nu}\,C_{2;l_{2}l_{2}}+(l_{1\perp}^{\mu}l_{2\perp}^{\nu}+l_{2\perp}^{\mu}l_{1\perp}^{\nu})\,C_{2;l_{1}l_{2}}\,.

Again, we can easily and directly extract the scalar functions

C_{2;l1l1}
,
C_{2;l2l2}
, and

C_{2;l1l2}
using the formulas

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp})^{2}}\,C_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\,l_{2\mu}l_{2\nu}\,,

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{2}l_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp})^{2}}\,C_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\,l_{1\mu}l_{1\nu}\,,

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{1}l_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp})^{2}}\,C_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\,l_{1\mu}l_{2\nu}\,,

\displaystyle C_{2;g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D-2}\,C_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\,g_{\perp;3;\mu\nu}\,.

(31)

Analytic expressions for these functions read

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\,\biggl[-l_{1}l_{2}\,B_{0}(s_{1})+(s_{2}+l_{1}l_{2})B_{0}(s_{3})-\frac{\lambda\,s_{2}}{2l_{1}l_{2}}\,C_{1;l_{1}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{2}l_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\,\biggl[-l_{1}l_{2}\,B_{0}(s_{2})+(s_{1}+l_{1}l_{2})B_{0}(s_{3})-\frac{\lambda\,s_{1}}{2l_{1}l_{2}}\,C_{1;l_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{1}l_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\,\biggl[-s_{1}\,B_{0}(s_{1})-s_{2}\,B_{0}(s_{2})+(s_{1}+s_{2}+2l_{1}l_{2})\,B_{0}(s_{3})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2l_{1}l_{2}}\,\Big(s_{1}C_{1;l_{1}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})+s_{2}C_{1;l_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\Big)\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle C_{2;g}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D-2}\,\biggl[B_{0}(s_{3})+\frac{\lambda}{4(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}\,\Big(s_{1}C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})+s_{2}C_{2;l_{2}l_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\Big)

(32)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2l_{1}l_{2}}\,C_{2;l_{1}l_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\biggr]\,.

Note, that the functions
C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

and
C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

are symmetric under exchange of first two arguments

s_{1}\leftrightarrow s_{2}
.
In Appendix
A
we present the solutions for
the scalar functions
C_{2;g}
,

C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}
,
C_{2;l_{2}l_{2}}
, and
C_{2;l_{1}l_{2}}
for
specific choice of momenta (including soft limits).
Finally, we note that the perpendicular basis vector
l_{i\perp}

can be easily expressed through the basis vectors
P
,
R
, and
T
.

Finally we present analytic results for triangle tensor rank-3 integral

\displaystyle C_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}k^{\alpha}}{k^{2}\,(k+l_{1})^{2}\,(k+l_{2})^{2}}

(33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp;l_{1\perp}}\,C_{3;gl_{1}}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp;l_{2\perp}}\,C_{3;gl_{2}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{l_{1\perp}l_{1\perp}l_{1\perp}\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{1}}+\{l_{2\perp}l_{2\perp}l_{2\perp}\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,C_{3;l_{2}l_{2}l_{2}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{l_{1\perp}l_{1\perp}l_{2\perp}\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{2}}+\{l_{2\perp}l_{2\perp}l_{1\perp}\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,C_{3;l_{2}l_{2}l_{1}}\,.

where

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp;l_{i\perp}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp;3}l_{i\perp}^{\alpha}+g^{\mu\alpha}_{\perp;3}l_{i\perp}^{\beta}+g^{\mu\beta}_{\perp;3}l_{i\perp}^{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\{AAA\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\{AAB\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}B^{\alpha}+A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}B^{\mu}+A^{\alpha}A^{\mu}B^{\nu}\,.

(34)

The scalar functions
C_{3;gl_{1}}
,

C_{3;gl_{2}}
,

C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{1}}
,
C_{3;l_{2}l_{2}l_{2}}
,
C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{2}}
, and

C_{3;l_{2}l_{2}l_{1}}
are given by

\displaystyle C_{3;gl_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4l_{1}l_{2}}{(D-2)\,\lambda(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}\,g_{\mu\nu;3}\,l_{2\alpha}\,C_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

(35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{l_{1}l_{2}}{(D-2)\,\lambda(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}\,\frac{B_{0}(l_{3}^{2})}{2}\,l_{2}(2l_{2}+l_{3})\,,

\displaystyle C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{64(l_{1}l_{2})^{3}}{\lambda^{3}(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}\,l_{2\mu}\,l_{2\nu}\,l_{2\alpha}\,C_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

(36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8(l_{1}l_{2})^{3}}{\lambda^{3}(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}\,\biggl[B_{0}(l_{2}^{2})\,\frac{D(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}-l_{1}^{2}l_{2}^{2}}{D-1}\,-\,B_{0}(l_{3}^{2})\,\frac{D(l_{1}l_{3})^{2}-l_{1}^{2}l_{3}^{2}}{D-1}\,

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle B_{0}(l_{3}^{2})\Big(l_{1}(2l_{1}-l_{3})\Big)^{2}\,-\,C_{2;l_{2}l_{2}}\,\frac{l_{1}^{2}\,\lambda^{2}(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}{4(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{64(l_{1}l_{2})^{3}}{\lambda^{3}(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}\,l_{2\mu}\,l_{2\nu}\,l_{1\alpha}\,C_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})

(37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8(l_{1}l_{2})^{3}}{\lambda^{3}(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}\,\biggl[B_{0}(l_{2}^{2})\,l_{2}^{4}\,-\,B_{0}(l_{3}^{2})\,\Big(l_{2}(2l_{2}+l3)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle C_{2;l_{1}l_{1}}\,\frac{l_{1}^{2}\,\lambda^{2}(l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2},l_{3}^{2})}{4(l_{1}l_{2})^{2}}\biggr]\,.

Here functions

C_{3;gl_{2}}
,
C_{3;l_{2}l_{2}l_{2}}
,
C_{3;l_{2}l_{2}l_{1}}
are
obtainded from functions

C_{3;gl_{1}}
,
C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{1}}
,
C_{3;l_{1}l_{1}l_{2}}

under replacements
l_{1}\to l_{2}
,
l_{2}\to l_{1}
,
l_{3}\to-l_{3}
.
As before, the perpendicular basis vector
l_{i\perp}

is easily expressed through the basis vectors
P
,
R
, and
T
.

2.3
Box integrals

A typical box integral is shown in Fig.
1
(d)
[the others are obtained by permutation of external momenta
in the propagators].

We start with the calculation of the scalar integral

\displaystyle D_{0}(s,u,Q^{2})\equiv I_{0125}=(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{1}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{5}}\,.

(38)

To pin down this integral we use Feynman
\alpha
-parametrization
(for calculation of fully massless integral with
Q^{2}=0
see
Ref.
Smirnov:2006ry
). The final results can be written
in terms of the Gauss hypergeometric function

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)

[see details of calculation in Appendix
B.1
].
The expression for the box integral reads
(see also Refs.
Duplancic:2000sk
;
Cachazo:2004zb
;
Ellis:2007qk
):

\displaystyle D_{0}(s,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{su}\ \Biggl[\pi^{\frac{D}{2}-2}\frac{\Gamma\Big(2-\frac{D}{2}\Big)\,\Gamma^{2}\Big(\frac{D}{2}-2\Big)}{\Gamma(D-4)}\Biggr]

(39)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Biggl[\Big(-\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{D}{2}-2}\ _{2}F_{1}\Big(1,\frac{D}{2}-2,\frac{D}{2}-1,-\frac{t}{u}\Big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big(-\frac{u}{\mu^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{D}{2}-2}\ _{2}F_{1}\Big(1,\frac{D}{2}-2,\frac{D}{2}-1,-\frac{t}{s}\Big)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Big(-\frac{Q^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\Big)^{\frac{D}{2}-2}\ _{2}F_{1}\Big(1,\frac{D}{2}-2,\frac{D}{2}-1,-\frac{Q^{2}t}{su}\Big)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{su}\ \Biggl[\frac{D-3}{D-4}\Biggr]\ \Biggl[B_{0}(s)\ _{2}F_{1}\Big(1,\frac{D}{2}-2,\frac{D}{2}-1,-\frac{t}{u}\Big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle B_{0}(u)\ _{2}F_{1}\Big(1,\frac{D}{2}-2,\frac{D}{2}-1,-\frac{t}{s}\Big)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle B_{0}(Q^{2})\ _{2}F_{1}\Big(1,\frac{D}{2}-2,\frac{D}{2}-1,-\frac{Q^{2}t}{su}\Big)\Biggr]\,.

Note that in the on-shell limit
Q^{2}=s+t+u=0
we
reproduce the result for the scalar box given in Ref.
Smirnov:2006ry
.

\varepsilon
expansion of the scalar box diagram is discussed in
detail in Appendix
B.1
. The results
for the other scalar box integrals are obtained via permutation
of Mandelstam variables
(s,t,u)
.
Here we display important symmetry properties of the scalar box integral:

\displaystyle D_{0}(s,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{0}(u,s,Q^{2})=I_{0125}\,,\

\displaystyle D_{0}(s,t,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{0}(t,s,Q^{2})=I_{0126}\,,\

\displaystyle D_{0}(u,t,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{0}(t,u,Q^{2})=I_{0235}\,.

(40)

Now we are in the position to derive the expressions for the tensorial
box integrals (with one, two, and three loop momentum vectors in numerator)
using Lorentz covariance in the orthogonal basis. The loop integrals
will be expanded in terms of scalar bubble and triangle PV functions.

We start with vector integrals:

\displaystyle D_{1}^{\mu}(s,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{5}}

(41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\mu}D_{1P}^{0125}+R^{\mu}D_{1R}^{0125}+T^{\mu}D_{1T}^{0125}\,,

\displaystyle D_{1}^{\mu}(s,t,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{6}}

(42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\mu}D_{1P}^{0126}+R^{\mu}D_{1R}^{0126}+T^{\mu}D_{1T}^{0126}\,,

\displaystyle D_{1}^{\mu}(t,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{3}\,\Delta_{5}}

(43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{\mu}D_{1P}^{0235}+R^{\mu}D_{1R}^{0235}+T^{\mu}D_{1T}^{0235}\,,

where
D_{1F}^{ijkl}
are the scalar functions, which are obtained
by contraction of the vector integrals
D_{1}^{\mu}(s_{1},s_{2},Q^{2})

with corresponding Lorentz structure.
In contrast to the original PV method, there remains no system
of equations to be solved.
E.g., the function
D_{1P}^{0125}
is simply fixed as

\displaystyle D_{1P}^{0125}=\frac{1}{P^{2}}\,P_{\mu}\cdot D_{1}^{\mu}(s,u,Q^{2})\,.

(44)

Then we use the fractioning identity

\displaystyle 2kP=\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\,.

(45)

to reduce the number of factors
in the denominator of
D_{1}^{\mu}(s,u,Q^{2})
.
Using Eq. (
45
) we get an expression for

D_{1P}^{0125}
as linear combination of known scalar triangle
integrals:

\displaystyle D_{1P}^{0125}=\frac{1}{2}\,\biggl[C_{0}^{025}-C_{0}^{015}\biggr]=\frac{1}{2}\,\biggl[C_{0}(u,Q^{2})-C_{0}(u)\biggr]\,.

(46)

By analogy we pin down all scalar functions
occurring in Eqs. (
41
)-(
43
),
which are listed in Appendix
B.2
.
Also there we give the set of the fractional identities
needed to simplify the box diagrams.

The tensor rank-2 box integrals read:

\displaystyle D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{5}}

(47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,D_{2g}^{0125}+\{PP\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PP}^{0125}+\{RR\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2RR}^{0125}+\{TT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2TT}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PR\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PR}^{0125}+\{PT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PT}^{0125}+\{RT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2RT}^{0125}\,,

\displaystyle D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s,t,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{6}}

(48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,D_{2g}^{0126}+\{PP\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PP}^{0126}+\{RR\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2RR}^{0126}+\{TT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2TT}^{0126}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PR\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PR}^{0126}+\{PT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PT}^{0126}+\{RT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2RT}^{0126}\,,

\displaystyle D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(t,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{3}\,\Delta_{5}}

(49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,D_{2g}^{0235}+\{PP\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PP}^{0235}+\{RR\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2RR}^{0235}+\{TT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2TT}^{0235}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PR\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PR}^{0235}+\{PT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2PT}^{0235}+\{RT\}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{2RT}^{0235}\,,

where

\{AA\}^{\mu\nu}=A^{\mu}A^{\nu}
and

\{AB\}^{\mu\nu}=A^{\mu}B^{\nu}+A^{\nu}B^{\mu}
.
We handle tensor rank-2 box integrals analogous to vector
integrals. In particular, we fix the scalar functions

D_{2F}^{ijkl}
occurring in the
expansions (
47
)-(
49
)
by multiplying the corresponding loop integral with the corresponding
Lorenz structures
g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
,
P^{\mu}P^{\nu}
,
R^{\mu}R^{\nu}
,
and
T^{\mu}T^{\nu}
, respectively.
We do not need to solve any system of equations as
in the original PV method. Again, as for vectorial
box integrals we use the fractioning identities
(see Appendix
B.2
).
In particular, the results for the
D_{2g}^{0125}
,

D_{2PP}^{0125}
,
D_{2PR}^{0125}
, and
D_{2TT}^{0125}

functions read:

\displaystyle D_{2PP}^{0125}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P^{4}}\,P_{\mu}P_{\nu}\cdot D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s,u,Q^{2})=\frac{1}{2s}\,\biggl[C_{1P}^{025}-C_{1P}^{015}\biggr]\,,

(50)

\displaystyle D_{2RR}^{0125}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R^{4}}\,R_{\mu}R_{\nu}\cdot D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s,u,Q^{2})=\frac{1}{2s}\,\biggl[C_{1R}^{025}+C_{1R}^{015}-2C_{1R}^{078}+C_{0}^{025}\biggr]\,,

(51)

\displaystyle D_{2TT}^{0125}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{4}}\,T_{\mu}T_{\nu}\cdot D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s,u,Q^{2})=-\frac{s}{2ut}\,\biggl[uD_{1T}^{0125}-C_{1T}^{015}\,\frac{u}{s}

(52)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle C_{1T}^{025}\,\frac{s+t}{s}+C_{1T}^{078}\,\frac{u-t}{s}\biggr]\,,

\displaystyle D_{2g}^{0125}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D-3}\,g_{\mu\nu}^{\perp}\cdot D_{2}^{\mu\nu}(s,u,Q^{2})=\frac{1}{D-3}\,\biggl[C_{0}^{078}-s\,D_{2PP}^{0125}

(53)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle s\,D_{2RR}^{0125}+\frac{ut}{s}\,D_{2TT}^{0125}\biggr]\,.

The tensorial rank-3 box integrals read:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptD_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(s,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}k^{\alpha}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{5}}=g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp P}\,D_{3gP}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp R}\,D_{3gR}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp T}\,D_{3gT}^{0125}

(54)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPP}^{0125}+\{RRR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRR}^{0125}+\{TTT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTT}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPR}^{0125}+\{PPT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPT}^{0125}+\{RRP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRP}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{RRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRT}^{0125}+\{TTP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTP}^{0125}+\{TTR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTR}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PRT}^{0125}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptD_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(s,t,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}k^{\alpha}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{6}}=g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp P}\,D_{3gP}^{0126}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp R}\,D_{3gR}^{0126}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp T}\,D_{3gT}^{0126}

(55)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPP}^{0126}+\{RRR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRR}^{0126}+\{TTT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTT}^{0126}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPR}^{0126}+\{PPT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPT}^{0126}+\{RRP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRP}^{0126}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{RRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRT}^{0126}+\{TTP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTP}^{0126}+\{TTR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTR}^{0126}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PRT}^{0126}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptD_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}(t,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}k^{\alpha}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{3}\,\Delta_{5}}=g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp P}\,D_{3gP}^{0235}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp R}\,D_{3gR}^{0235}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp T}\,D_{3gT}^{0235}

(56)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPP}^{0235}+\{RRR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRR}^{0235}+\{TTT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTT}^{0235}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPR}^{0235}+\{PPT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PPT}^{0235}+\{RRP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRP}^{0126}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{RRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3RRT}^{0235}+\{TTP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTP}^{0235}+\{TTR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3TTR}^{0235}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,D_{3PRT}^{0235}\,,

where

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha}_{\perp A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}A^{\alpha}+g^{\mu\alpha}_{\perp}A^{\nu}+g^{\nu\alpha}_{\perp}A^{\mu}\,,

\displaystyle\{AAA\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}\,,

\displaystyle\{AAB\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}B^{\alpha}+A^{\mu}A^{\alpha}B^{\nu}+A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}B^{\mu}\,,

(57)

\displaystyle\{ABC\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}B^{\nu}C^{\alpha}+A^{\mu}B^{\alpha}C^{\nu}+A^{\nu}B^{\mu}C^{\alpha}+A^{\nu}B^{\alpha}C^{\mu}+A^{\alpha}B^{\mu}C^{\nu}+A^{\alpha}B^{\nu}C^{\mu}\,.

As before the scalar functions defining expansion of rank-3 box integrals
are simply fixed by contraction of the integral

D_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}
with corresponding Lorentz structure:

\displaystyle D_{3AAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mu}A_{\nu}A_{\alpha}}{A^{6}}\,D_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,,

(58)

\displaystyle D_{3AAB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mu}A_{\nu}B_{\alpha}}{A^{4}B^{2}}\,D_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,,

(59)

\displaystyle D_{3ABC}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mu}B_{\nu}C_{\alpha}}{A^{2}B^{2}C^{2}}\,D_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

(60)

\displaystyle D_{3gA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{\mu\nu}^{\perp}A_{\alpha}}{(D-3)A^{2}}\,D_{3}^{\mu\nu\alpha}\,,

(61)

where
A,B,C=P,R,T
.
We list all scalar functions occurring in the expansion of tensorial
rank-2 and rank-3 box integrals in Appendix
B.3
.

Extension on tensorial box integral with higher rank is straightforward.
One should just specify the Lorentz structure of corresponding
integral and then fix the scalar functions on which the integral is
expanded. In particular, decomposition of the rank-4 box inegral
with denominators
\Delta_{0}
,
\Delta_{1}
,
\Delta_{2}
, and

\Delta_{5}
reads:

\displaystyle D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}(s,u,Q^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi^{2})^{-1}\,\mu^{4-D}\,\int d^{D}k\,\frac{k^{\mu}k^{\nu}k^{\alpha}k^{\beta}}{\Delta_{0}\,\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{5}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp}\,D_{4gg}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;PP}\,D_{4g;PP}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;RR}\,D_{4g;RR}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;TT}\,D_{4g;TT}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;PR}\,D_{4g;PR}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;PT}\,D_{4g;PT}^{0125}+g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;RT}\,D_{4g;RT}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPPP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4PPPP}^{0125}+\{RRRR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4RRRR}^{0125}+\{TTTT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4TTTT}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPPR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4PPPR}^{0125}+\{PPPT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4PPPT}^{0125}+\{RRRP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4RRRP}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{RRRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4RRRT}^{0125}+\{TTTP\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4TTTP}^{0125}+\{TTTR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4TTTR}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{PPRR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4PPRR}^{0125}+\{PPTT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4PPTT}^{0125}+\{PPRT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4PPRT}^{0125}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\{RRTT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4RRTT}^{0125}+\{RRPT\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4RRPT}^{0125}+\{TTPR\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,D_{4TTPR}^{0125}\,,

where

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,g^{\alpha\beta}_{\perp}\,+\,g^{\mu\alpha}_{\perp}\,g^{\nu\beta}_{\perp}\,+\,g^{\mu\beta}_{\perp}\,g^{\nu\alpha}_{\perp}\,,

(63)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,\{AA\}^{\alpha\beta}\,+\,g^{\mu\alpha}_{\perp}\,\{AA\}^{\nu\beta}\,+\,g^{\mu\beta}_{\perp}\,\{AA\}^{\nu\alpha}\,,

(64)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}_{\perp;AB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\,\{AB\}^{\alpha\beta}\,+\,g^{\mu\alpha}_{\perp}\,\{AB\}^{\nu\beta}\,+\,g^{\mu\beta}_{\perp}\,\{AB\}^{\nu\alpha}

(65)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}_{\perp}\,\{AB\}^{\mu\nu}\,+\,g^{\nu\beta}_{\perp}\,\{AB\}^{\mu\alpha}\,+\,g^{\nu\alpha}_{\perp}\,\{AB\}^{\nu\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\{AAAA\}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}A^{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\{AAAB\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}B^{\beta}+A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}A^{\beta}B^{\mu}+A^{\alpha}A^{\beta}A^{\mu}B^{\nu}+A^{\beta}A^{\mu}A^{\nu}B^{\alpha}\,,

(66)

\displaystyle\{AABC\}^{\mu\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mu}A^{\nu}B^{\alpha}C^{\beta}+A^{\mu}A^{\nu}B^{\beta}C^{\alpha}+A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}B^{\beta}C^{\mu}+A^{\nu}A^{\alpha}B^{\mu}C^{\beta}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle A^{\alpha}A^{\beta}B^{\mu}C^{\nu}+A^{\alpha}A^{\beta}B^{\nu}C^{\mu}\,.

Again, all scalar functions defining expansion of rank-4 box integrals
are simply fixed by contraction of the integral

D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}
with corresponding Lorentz structure:

\displaystyle D_{4gg}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(D-3)(D-1)}\,g_{\mu\nu}^{\perp}\,g_{\alpha\beta}^{\perp}\cdot D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,,

(67)

\displaystyle D_{4gg;AA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(D-3)A^{4}}\,g_{\mu\nu}^{\perp}\,A_{\alpha}A_{\beta}\cdot D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,,

(68)

\displaystyle D_{4gg;AB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(D-3)A^{2}B^{2}}\,g_{\mu\nu}^{\perp}\,A_{\alpha}B_{\beta}\cdot D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,,

(69)

\displaystyle D_{4AAAA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mu}A_{\nu}A_{\alpha}A^{\beta}}{A^{8}}\,D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,,

(70)

\displaystyle D_{4AAAB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mu}A_{\nu}A_{\alpha}B^{\beta}}{A^{6}B^{2}}\,D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,,

(71)

\displaystyle D_{4AABC}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A_{\mu}A_{\nu}B_{\alpha}C^{\beta}}{A^{4}B^{2}C^{2}}\,D_{4}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,.

(72)

3
Angular integrals in
D
dimensions

In this section we discuss new ideas concerning angular integration in QCD.
We extend the known closed results for massless and single massive two denominator
integrals in
D
dimensions derived
in Refs.
vanNeerven:1985xr
;
Beenakker:1988bq
;
Somogyi:2011ir

to the double massive case.
Furthermore we investigate novel analytic and algebraic properties of angular integrals.
An all-order
\varepsilon
-expansion is given for all angular integrals with up to
two denominators. A supplementary geometric picture for partial fractioning is developed
in Appendix
D

which provides a new rotational invariant algorithm to reduce the number of denominators
and also proves to be useful for the double massive integral.

3.1
Generalized two particle phase space in
D
dimensions

To set the stage, we consider the Lorentz invariant phase space integral (PSI)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{PS}_{2,P}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D-1}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D-1}2k_{1}^{0}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D-1}k_{2}}{(2\pi)^{D-1}2k_{2}^{0}}\,(2\pi)^{D}\delta^{D}\left(P-k_{1}-k_{2}\right)\,.

(73)

where
P=p_{1}+p_{2}+\dots+p_{n}
is the sum of some fixed momenta.
One might think of this PSI
potentially as a part of some larger PSI.

P
shall be time-like and future-pointing such that there exists a rest frame of
P

with
P^{0}>0
. The
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are assumed as momenta of massless particles,
i.e.
k_{1}^{0}=|{\bf k_{1}}|
and
k_{2}^{0}=|{\bf k_{2}}|
.
But we should stress that, in general,
final particles are not neccessary massless and we can deal with
massive particles (see also Ref.
Mirkes:1992hu
).

Applying the identity

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{D}k\,\delta(k^{2})\,\theta(E_{k})\,f(k)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D-1}k}{2k^{0}}\,f(k)\,,

(74)

for the integral over
k_{2}
and evaluating it using the
D
dimensional
\delta
-function,
the phase space is written as

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{PS}_{2,P}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{D-1}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{D-2}|{\bf k_{1}}|}\,\delta\left((P_{1}-k_{1})^{2}\right)\theta(P^{0}-k_{1}^{0})\,.

(75)

By choosing the rest frame of
P
we have

P=(\sqrt{P^{2}},\mathbf{0})
and
k_{1}=(|\mathbf{k}|,\mathbf{k})

leading to
(P-k_{1})^{2}=2\sqrt{P^{2}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{P^{2}}}{2}-|\mathbf{k}|\right)
.
Employing spherical coordinates in this frame and evaluating the radial integral,
we get

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{PS}_{2,P}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(2\pi)^{D-2}\sqrt{P^{2}}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}|\mathbf{k}|\,|\mathbf{k}|^{D-3}\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{D-2}\,\delta\left(|\mathbf{k}|-\frac{\sqrt{P^{2}}}{2}\right)\theta\left(\sqrt{P^{2}}-|\mathbf{k}|\right)\,

\displaystyle=\frac{(P^{2})^{\frac{D-4}{2}}}{2(4\pi)^{D-2}}\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{D-2}\,.

(76)

Notice that the
\theta
-function is trivially satisfied since
P^{0}>0
.
If we are in a situation, where the function we want to integrate over the phase space,
does only depend on two angles, the remaining
D-4
dimensional spherical integral
can be integrated out (see
details of spherical integration in dimensional regularization in Appendix
C
).
Defining

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\equiv\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\sin^{1-2\varepsilon}\theta_{1}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\sin^{-2\varepsilon}\theta_{2}\,

(77)

with
D=4-2\varepsilon
and using the area formula for
n
dimensional spheres

\displaystyle\Omega_{n}=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)}=\frac{2^{n}\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(n)}=\frac{2(4\pi)^{\frac{n}{2}}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{n}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(1+n)}

(78)

the angular integral becomes

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{D-2}=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{D-4}\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\longrightarrow\Omega_{D-4}\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}=\frac{2\,(4\pi)^{-\varepsilon}\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)}\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\,.

(79)

Therefore,
we find the well known formula for the two-body phase space in
D=4-2\varepsilon
dimensions
in the rest frame of
P

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{PS}_{2,P}=\frac{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}{(4\pi)^{2-\varepsilon}\,\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)}\left(P^{2}\right)^{-\varepsilon}\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\,.

(80)

If the integrand of the PSI is a rational function of the angular dependent
Mandelstam variables, then it can be further simplified and
reduced to a set of master integrals by partial fraction decomposition.
In Appendix
D
we discuss in details a novel geometric approach
developed for this task. There, we considered the double real corrections
in Drell-Yan process as an illustration of how the partial fractioning works (see Appendix
D.6
).

3.2
Analytic evaluation of angular integrals in
D
dimension

We introduce a set of basis integrals to which all of the Neerven integrals can be reduced,
but that are characterized by properties that are invariant under rotation rather than
the parameters
a
,
b
,
A
,
B
, and
C
from Eq. (
1
).
They will now depend on the variables
v_{ij}=v_{i}\,\cdot\,v_{j}
,
which are all invariant under change of coordinates.
1
1

1

Note that this more natural normalization
is different than that used by Somogyi
Somogyi:2011ir
, which is taylored
to the Mellin-Barnes representations.

We define

Zero-denominator integral:

\displaystyle I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\,.

(81)

One-denominator massless integral:

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\ \dfrac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}}\,,\qquad v_{11}=0\,.

(82)

One-denominator massive integral:

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\ \dfrac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}}\,,\qquad v_{11}\neq 0\,.

(83)

Two-denominator massless integral:

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j,l}(v_{12};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\ \dfrac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}\,(v_{2}\cdot k)^{l}}\,,\qquad v_{11}=v_{22}=0\,.

(84)

Two-denominator single-massive integral:

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{j,l}(v_{12},v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\ \dfrac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}\,(v_{2}\cdot k)^{l}}\,,\qquad v_{11}\neq 0,\,v_{22}=0\,.

(85)

Two-denominator double-massive integral:

\displaystyle I^{(2)}_{j,l}(v_{12},v_{11},v_{22};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\ \dfrac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}\,(v_{2}\cdot k)^{l}}\,,\qquad v_{11}\neq 0,\,v_{22}\neq 0\,.

(86)

Here

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\sin^{1-2\varepsilon}\theta_{1}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\sin^{-2\varepsilon}\theta_{2}\,,

(87)

is the integration measure
in a frame with
k=(1,\dots,\cos\theta_{2}\sin\theta_{1},\cos\theta_{1})

[the dots represent irrelevant angles since
\mathbf{v_{1}}
and
\mathbf{v_{2}}
lie in the
(x_{D-1},x_{D})
-plane].
The upper number in parenthesis gives the number of masses involved and the number of lower indices gives
the number of different denominators. All appearing
D
-vectors are normalized such that their 0-component
is equal to 1.

We will calculate the integrals one by one which has the benefit that we gradually increase the difficulty of
the calculation and can introduce the employed methods one by one.

3.2.1
Integral without denominator

We start with the basic integral
I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)
without any denominator

\displaystyle I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\sin^{1-2\varepsilon}\theta_{1}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\sin^{-2\varepsilon}\theta_{2}\,.

(88)

Changing the variables in both integrals as
\cos\theta_{i}=1-2t_{i}
with
d\theta_{i}\,\sin\theta_{i}=2dt_{i}
and

\sin\theta_{i}=2\sqrt{t_{i}\,(1-t_{i})}
we rewrite the integral
I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)
in the form

\displaystyle I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)=2^{1-4\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\,t_{1}^{-\varepsilon}(1-t_{1})^{-\varepsilon}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,t_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(1-t_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}\,,

(89)

containing the product of two integral representations of the beta function

\displaystyle B(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma(x)\Gamma(y)}{\Gamma(x+y)}=\int\limits_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{x-1}\,(1-t)^{y-1}\,.

(90)

Taking into account Eq. (
90
) we finally get:

\displaystyle I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)=2^{1-4\varepsilon}\frac{\Gamma^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)}\frac{\Gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)}\,.

(91)

Using the Legendre duplication formula

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2^{-2\varepsilon}}\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)\,,

(92)

I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)
is further simplified to

\displaystyle I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)=2\pi\,\frac{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)}=\frac{2\pi}{1-2\varepsilon}\,.

(93)

The latter factor will occur as universal multiplier in all other angular integrals.

3.2.2
Massless integral with one denominator

In the calculations of the angular integrals we follow ideas proposed and
developed in Ref.
Somogyi:2011ir
. In particular,
for the calculation of the massless one denominator integral
I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)

we rotate the frame such that
the unit vector
\mathbf{v_{1}}
points in the
x_{D}
-direction, i.e.
v_{1}=(1,\mathbf{0}_{D-3},0,1)
,

k=(1,\mathbf{0}_{D-3},\sin\theta_{1}\cos\theta_{2},\cos\theta_{1})
,
and
v_{1}\cdot k=1-\cos\theta_{1}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\frac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\frac{\sin^{1-2\varepsilon}\theta_{1}}{(1-\cos\theta_{1})^{j}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\sin^{-2\varepsilon}\theta_{2}\,.

(94)

Next we proceed by analogy with the integral
I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)
: employing the same change of variables
and using the integral representation (
90
) for the beta function.
We find:

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=2^{1-j-4\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\,t_{1}^{-j-\varepsilon}(1-t_{1})^{-\varepsilon}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,t_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(1-t_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=2^{1-j-4\varepsilon}\frac{\Gamma(1-j-\varepsilon)\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(2-j-2\varepsilon)}\frac{\Gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)}\,.

(95)

Separating the factor
I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)
and using the
Pochhammer symbol

\displaystyle(x)_{n}=\frac{\Gamma(x+n)}{\Gamma(x)}

(96)

yields

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{1-2\varepsilon}2^{-j}\frac{\Gamma(1-j-\varepsilon)\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)\Gamma(2-j-2\varepsilon)}=I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)\,\frac{(2-j-2\varepsilon)_{j}}{2^{j}(1-j-\varepsilon)_{j}}\,.

(97)

In this form
I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)

is a manifestly rational function
of
\varepsilon
if
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, such that apparently
no polygamma constants will clutter the
\varepsilon
-expansion.

3.2.3
Massive integral with one denominator

We choose the same frame as in the massless case with
\mathbf{v}_{1}
in
x_{D}
direction:

v=(1,\mathbf{0}_{D-3},0,\beta)
and
v_{1}\cdot k=1-\beta\cos\theta_{1}

with
v_{11}\neq 0
and
\beta\equiv\sqrt{1-v_{11}}\neq 1
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046ptI^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\frac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\frac{\sin^{1-2\varepsilon}\theta_{1}}{(1-\beta\cos\theta_{1})^{j}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}\sin^{-2\varepsilon}\theta_{2}\,.

(98)

The
\cos\theta_{1}=1-2t
substitution now gives

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt1-\beta\cos\theta_{1}=(1-\beta)\left(1+\frac{2\beta}{1-\beta}\,t\right)\,,

(99)

thus

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046ptI^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{1-4\varepsilon}}{(1-\beta)^{j}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\,t_{1}^{-\varepsilon}(1-t_{1})^{-\varepsilon}\left(1+\frac{2\beta}{1-\beta}\,t\right)^{-j}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,t_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(1-t_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}\,.

(100)

Next, using the integral representation for the
Gauss hypergeometric function
(Euler formula)

\phantom{}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)=\frac{\Gamma(c)}{\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c-b)}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,t^{b-1}\,(1-t)^{c-b-1}\,(1-zt)^{-a}\,,

(101)

in case of the integral over
t_{1}

and isolating the factor
I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)
one gets:

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{1-4\varepsilon}}{(1-\beta)^{j}}\frac{\Gamma^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(2-2\varepsilon)}\,\phantom{}_{2}F_{1}\left(j,1-\varepsilon,2-2\varepsilon,-\frac{2\beta}{1-\beta}\right)\frac{\Gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}{\Gamma(1-2\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\frac{I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)}{(1-\beta)^{j}}\,\phantom{}_{2}F_{1}\left(j,1-\varepsilon,2-2\varepsilon,-\frac{2\beta}{1-\beta}\right)\,.

(102)

Here we should make two important remarks on the
Gauss function:
(1) the Gauss function with integer coefficients occurring in
Eq. (
102
)
is well suited for the
\varepsilon-
expansion;
(2) the Gauss function can be defined by the power series using
Pochhammer symbol
as:

\phantom{}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(a)_{n}\,(b)_{n}}{(c)_{n}}\,\frac{z^{n}}{n!}\,,

(103)

which is manifestly symmetric under interchange of the first and second arguments
a\leftrightarrow b
.

The integral
I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)
will be a cornerstone for consideration of further integrals.
For this purpose it is feasible to deduce a Mellin-Barnes representation of the integral,
which has a particularly simple dependence on
v_{11}
. To this end we first employ
the quadratic hypergeometric transformation

\phantom{}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,2b,x)=\left(1-\frac{x}{2}\right)^{-a}\,\phantom{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{a}{2},\frac{a+1}{2},b+\frac{1}{2},\left(\frac{x}{2-x}\right)^{2}\right)\,,

(104)

to convert
I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)
into

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)=I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)\,\phantom{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{j}{2},\frac{j+1}{2},\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon,1-v_{11}\right)\,.

(105)

In a second step we use the Mellin-Barnes representation

\phantom{}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(c)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\Gamma(c-a)\Gamma(c-b)}

\displaystyle\times\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi i}\,\Gamma(a+z)\Gamma(b+z)\Gamma(c-a-b-z)\Gamma(-z)(1-x)^{z}\,.

(106)

This results in

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{j}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{j+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{3-j}{2}-\varepsilon\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{2-j}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}

\displaystyle\times\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi i}\Gamma\left(\frac{j}{2}+z\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{j+1}{2}+z\right)\Gamma\left(1-j-\varepsilon-z\right)\Gamma\left(-z\right)v_{11}^{z}\,.

(107)

Applying the Legendre duplication formula
on each gamma function that contains a half integer, results
in the Mellin-Barnes representation

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptI^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)}\frac{(2-j-2\varepsilon)_{j}}{2^{j}\Gamma(j)}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi i}\Gamma(j+2z)\Gamma(1-j-\varepsilon-z)\Gamma(-z)\left(\frac{v_{11}}{4}\right)^{z}\,.

(108)

This form will prove valuable for further calculations due to its simple dependence on
v_{11}
.

As a crosscheck of our calculations we are able to demonstrate that applying the limit
v_{11}\rightarrow 0

we get

I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11},\varepsilon)\overset{v_{11}\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)
. For this purpose
the form (
105
) is well suited. In particular, using the
Gauss theorem

\phantom{}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,1)=\frac{\Gamma(c)\Gamma(c-a-b)}{\Gamma(c-a)\Gamma(c-b)}\quad\text{for }\mathrm{Re}(c)>\mathrm{Re}(a+b)\,,

(109)

and the Legendre duplication formula one gets

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{j}(0,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)\,\phantom{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{j}{2},\frac{j+1}{2},\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon,1\right)

\displaystyle=I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)\,\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon\right)\Gamma(1-j-\varepsilon)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{3-j}{2}-\varepsilon\right)\Gamma\left(1-\frac{j}{2}-\varepsilon\right)}

\displaystyle=I^{(0)}(\varepsilon)\,\frac{(2-j-2\varepsilon)_{j}}{2^{j}\,(1-j-\varepsilon)_{j}}=I^{(0)}_{j}(\varepsilon)\,.

(110)

3.2.4
Massless integral with two denominators

For the two denominator case there is the issue that the
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
integrals do no
longer factorize. Therefore, it is feasible to disregard a direct
t
-substitution and
favor an approach based on Feynman parametrization to combine the denominators.
For the moment we assume
j,l\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.
Taking advantage of the linearity of the propagator this results in

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j,l}(v_{12};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\frac{1}{(v_{1}\cdot k)^{j}(v_{2}\cdot k)^{l}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B(j,l)}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,x_{1}^{j-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,x_{2}^{l-1}\,\delta(1-x_{1}-x_{2})

\displaystyle\times\int\mathrm{d}\Omega_{k_{1}k_{2}}\frac{1}{((x_{1}v_{1}+x_{2}v_{2})\cdot k)^{j+l}}\,.

(111)

We introduce the vector
v\equiv x_{1}v_{1}+x_{2}v_{2}
and rotate the coordinate system such that

\mathbf{v}
points in the
x_{D}
-direction.
In this frame it holds
v=(1,\mathbf{0}_{D-3},0,\sqrt{1-2x_{1}x_{2}v_{12}})
,
since in the massless case we have
v_{11}=v_{22}=0
such that

v^{2}=(x_{1}v_{1}+x_{2}v_{2})^{2}=2x_{1}x_{2}v_{12}
.
Thus, we can write

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j,l}(v_{12};\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(j+l)}{\Gamma(j)\Gamma(l)}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,x_{1}^{j-1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\,x_{2}^{l-1}\delta(1-x_{1}-x_{2})I^{(1)}_{j+l}(2x_{1}x_{2}v_{12})\,.

(112)

Employing the Mellin-Barnes representation of
I^{(1)}_{j}(v_{11};\varepsilon)
from
Eq. (
108
) brings this into the form

\displaystyle I^{(0)}_{j,l}(v_{12};\varepsilon)
